The full benchmark with the backwards search has finished. It's still a clear win overall, with some costs in specific cases.

Geometric mean, new / old (lower is faster)

Path Type na_rm = FALSE na_rm = TRUE
across double 0.29 0.40
across int 0.34 0.30
across logical 0.36 0.34
lane double 0.41 0.43
lane int 0.39 0.69
lane logical 0.39 0.67

Compared with the early-exit search

  • Short lanes (10 x 1e6 along axis 1): double improves from 1.29x to 0.62x. Int and logical improve from about 1.3x to about 0.94x.

  • Long int and logical lanes with na_rm = TRUE give back some speed (0.43x becomes 0.69x), because the search now always scans the whole lane.

Cases that are still slower than before

  • Short int and logical lanes are 1.1x to 1.6x slower in 8 of 24 cases. The worst is int min, na_rm = FALSE, with 10% NA: 6.3 ms becomes 10.2 ms. The old loop was fast there because of the "best is missing" branch I described earlier: once a lane hit NA, it skipped the rest of the lane for free.

  • long_ax2 (runs of 10 across lanes) is 0.8x to 1.1x. Each run is too short for the vector loop to pay off.

Everything else is 2x to 12x faster.

I haven't made the lane simplification yet; I'm waiting for your answer. If you want it, I'll make it and run the same benchmark against this version. That will show whether always computing the missing flag costs anything with na_rm = TRUE.

Davis

Why iterate backwards

Claude

Because the search no longer stops at the first match, it visits every element. Each match overwrites out_elt, so the last match it sees is the one that sticks. Going backwards, the last match seen is the first position in the lane, which is the tie rule we want (the same as which.max()):

for (i = end - 1; i >= start; --i, --position) {
  out_elt = v_x[i] == best ? position : out_elt;
}

Going forwards with the same line would return the last tie instead. To get the first tie going forwards, you need a second check so that later matches don't overwrite an earlier one:

for (i = start; i < end; ++i, ++position) {
  out_elt = (v_x[i] == best && out_elt == NA) ? position : out_elt;
}

That's an extra comparison against out_elt on every element. It also puts that comparison on the chain from one step to the next, since each step now reads the out_elt the previous step wrote. Going backwards avoids both, so it's the cheapest branch-free form.

I didn't benchmark the forward version with the extra check. I chose backwards from this reasoning, not from a measurement.

Davis

Try making the lane macro smaller

Claude

read <- function(label) {
  files <- Sys.glob(sprintf("/tmp/locate-small-%s-*.csv", label))
  runs <- lapply(files, read.csv)
  out <- runs[[1]]
  out$median_ms <- do.call(pmin, lapply(runs, `[[`, "median_ms"))
  out$build <- NULL
  out
}

back <- read("back")
small <- read("small")
keys <- c("type", "missing", "shape", "op", "na_rm")
both <- merge(back, small, by = keys, suffixes = c("_back", "_small"))
both$ratio <- both$median_ms_small / both$median_ms_back
both$path <- ifelse(grepl("ax1$", both$shape), "lane", "across")

geomean <- function(r) exp(mean(log(r)))
options(width = 200)

cat("Geometric mean ratio (small / back) by path, type, na_rm\n")
print(aggregate(ratio ~ path + type + na_rm, both, geomean), digits = 3)

cat("\nLane cases outside 0.95 to 1.05\n")
lane <- both[both$path == "lane" & (both$ratio < 0.95 | both$ratio > 1.05), ]
print(lane[order(lane$ratio), ], row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)

The smaller macro costs nothing overall. The one real cost is a handful of int and logical min cases, which are already well under a millisecond.

Geometric mean, smaller / previous

  • Across lanes: 0.996 to 1.005 for every type, so unchanged, as expected since that path didn't change.

  • Lanes with na_rm = TRUE: 0.99 to 1.01. Short lanes (10 elements) actually got about 10% faster.

  • Lanes with na_rm = FALSE: double 1.00, int 1.14, logical 1.08.

Where the cost is

All of it is min on int or logical with na_rm = FALSE. These used to run a plain min, where NA simply wins. Now they use the same reducer as na_rm = TRUE, which first swaps NA for INT_MAX (or 1 for logical). That extra step makes:

  • 1.1x to 1.14x slower with no missing values (3.5 ms becomes 4.0 ms on 1e7 elements),

  • 1.4x to 1.7x slower with 10% NA (0.63 ms becomes 1.06 ms).

Even the worst case is still about 3.7x faster than before this PR (3.97 ms → 1.06 ms).

I'd keep the smaller version. Getting that back means two lane calls again for int and logical min, which is exactly the complexity you wanted gone.

Nothing in this round is committed. The working tree has:

  • src/locate.c and src/decl/locate-decl.h: the new lane and across kernels.

  • tests/testthat/test-locate.R: the signed zero test.

Do you want me to commit and push it to the PR? Or would you rather look at the diff first?

static void rray_locate_max_int(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_obj* out
) {
  if (rray_run_iterator_stride(it, 0) == 0) {
    RRAY_LOCATE_LANE(
      int,
      r_int_cbegin,
      rray_int_is_missing,
      -INT_MAX,
      rray_max_int_one_na_rm
    );
  } else {
    if (na_rm) {
      RRAY_LOCATE_ACROSS(
        int,
        r_int_cbegin,
        r_int_begin,
        R_TYPE_integer,
        rray_int_is_missing,
        r_globals.na_int,
        rray_locate_max_int_one_na_rm
      );
    } else {
      RRAY_LOCATE_ACROSS(
        int,
        r_int_cbegin,
        r_int_begin,
        R_TYPE_integer,
        rray_int_is_missing,
        -INT_MAX,
        rray_locate_max_int_one
      );
    }
  }
}
static inline bool rray_locate_max_int_one(int x, int best) {
  return bool_bitwise_and(
    !rray_int_is_missing(best),
    bool_bitwise_or(rray_int_is_missing(x), x > best)
  );
}

static inline bool rray_locate_max_int_one_na_rm(int x, int best) {
  return x > best;
}

static inline bool rray_locate_max_dbl_one(double x, double best) {
  return bool_bitwise_or(rray_dbl_is_missing(x), x > best);
}

static inline bool rray_locate_max_dbl_one_na_rm(double x, double best) {
  return bool_bitwise_and(!rray_dbl_is_missing(x), !(x <= best));
}

static inline bool rray_locate_min_lgl_one(int x, int best) {
  return x < best;
}

static inline bool rray_locate_min_lgl_one_na_rm(int x, int best) {
  return bool_bitwise_and(
    !rray_lgl_is_missing(x),
    bool_bitwise_or(rray_lgl_is_missing(best), x < best)
  );
}

static inline bool rray_locate_min_int_one(int x, int best) {
  return x < best;
}

static inline bool rray_locate_min_int_one_na_rm(int x, int best) {
  return bool_bitwise_and(
    !rray_int_is_missing(x),
    bool_bitwise_or(rray_int_is_missing(best), x < best)
  );
}

static inline bool rray_locate_min_dbl_one(double x, double best) {
  return bool_bitwise_or(rray_dbl_is_missing(x), x < best);
}

static inline bool rray_locate_min_dbl_one_na_rm(double x, double best) {
  return bool_bitwise_and(!rray_dbl_is_missing(x), !(x >= best));
}

static inline int rray_locate_min_lgl_lane(int best, int x) {
  x = rray_lgl_is_missing(x) ? 1 : x;
  return x < best ? x : best;
}

static inline int rray_locate_min_int_lane(int best, int x) {
  x = rray_int_is_missing(x) ? INT_MAX : x;
  return x < best ? x : best;
}
Bash: sed -n '/^#define RRAY_LOCATE_LANE/,/^static void rray_locate_max_lgl(/p' src/locate.c | sed … 
sed -n '/^#define RRAY_LOCATE_LANE/,/^static void rray_locate_max_lgl(/p' src/locate.c | sed '$d' | sed 's/ *\\$//'
#define RRAY_LOCATE_LANE(CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, IS_MISSING, INIT, REDUCE)
  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);
  int* v_out = r_int_begin(out);

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(it); rray_run_iterator_next2(it)) {
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(it);
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(it);
    const r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 0);

    CTYPE best = INIT;
    bool any_missing = false;

    for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
      const CTYPE x_elt = v_x[i];
      best = REDUCE(best, x_elt);
      any_missing = bool_bitwise_or(any_missing, IS_MISSING(x_elt));
    }

    int out_elt = r_globals.na_int;

    if (na_rm || !any_missing) {
      int position = (int) rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 1) + (int) (end - start);

      for (r_ssize i = end - 1; i >= start; --i, --position) {
        out_elt = v_x[i] == best ? position : out_elt;
      }
    }

    v_out[out_loc] = out_elt;
  }

#define RRAY_LOCATE_ACROSS(
  CTYPE,
  CONST_DEREF,
  DEREF,
  TYPE,
  IS_MISSING,
  INIT,
  ONE
)
  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);
  int* v_out = r_int_begin(out);
  const r_ssize out_size = r_length(out);

  r_obj* best = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(TYPE, out_size));
  CTYPE* v_best = DEREF(best);

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    v_best[i] = INIT;
    v_out[i] = 1;
  }

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(it); rray_run_iterator_next2(it)) {
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(it);
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(it);

    r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 0);
    const int position = (int) rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 1) + 1;

    for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i, ++out_loc) {
      const CTYPE x_elt = v_x[i];
      const CTYPE best_elt = v_best[out_loc];
      const int out_elt = v_out[out_loc];
      const bool is_better = ONE(x_elt, best_elt);
      v_best[out_loc] = is_better ? x_elt : best_elt;
      v_out[out_loc] = is_better ? position : out_elt;
    }
  }

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    const int out_elt = v_out[i];
    v_out[i] = IS_MISSING(v_best[i]) ? r_globals.na_int : out_elt;
  }

  FREE(1);

Here's the core of the change, with the trailing \ removed from the macros so they're easier to read.

Lane path (reducing axis 1): a vectorized max or min, then a branch-free backwards search for the first match.

#define RRAY_LOCATE_LANE(CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, IS_MISSING, INIT, REDUCE)
  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);
  int* v_out = r_int_begin(out);

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(it); rray_run_iterator_next2(it)) {
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(it);
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(it);
    const r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 0);

    CTYPE best = INIT;
    bool any_missing = false;

    for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
      const CTYPE x_elt = v_x[i];
      best = REDUCE(best, x_elt);
      any_missing = bool_bitwise_or(any_missing, IS_MISSING(x_elt));
    }

    int out_elt = r_globals.na_int;

    if (na_rm || !any_missing) {
      int position = (int) rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 1) + (int) (end - start);

      for (r_ssize i = end - 1; i >= start; --i, --position) {
        out_elt = v_x[i] == best ? position : out_elt;
      }
    }

    v_out[out_loc] = out_elt;
  }

Across path (reducing any other axis): every value is read into a local before either store, which is what lets it vectorize. The stride is always 1 here, so out_loc just steps by 1.

#define RRAY_LOCATE_ACROSS(CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, DEREF, TYPE, IS_MISSING, INIT, ONE)
  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);
  int* v_out = r_int_begin(out);
  const r_ssize out_size = r_length(out);

  r_obj* best = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(TYPE, out_size));
  CTYPE* v_best = DEREF(best);

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    v_best[i] = INIT;
    v_out[i] = 1;
  }

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(it); rray_run_iterator_next2(it)) {
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(it);
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(it);

    r_ssize out_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 0);
    const int position = (int) rray_run_iterator_loc(it, 1) + 1;

    for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i, ++out_loc) {
      const CTYPE x_elt = v_x[i];
      const CTYPE best_elt = v_best[out_loc];
      const int out_elt = v_out[out_loc];
      const bool is_better = ONE(x_elt, best_elt);
      v_best[out_loc] = is_better ? x_elt : best_elt;
      v_out[out_loc] = is_better ? position : out_elt;
    }
  }

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    const int out_elt = v_out[i];
    v_out[i] = IS_MISSING(v_best[i]) ? r_globals.na_int : out_elt;
  }

  FREE(1);

One kernel. The other five follow the same shape.

static void rray_locate_max_int(
  r_obj* x,
  bool na_rm,
  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
  r_obj* out
) {
  if (rray_run_iterator_stride(it, 0) == 0) {
    RRAY_LOCATE_LANE(
      int,
      r_int_cbegin,
      rray_int_is_missing,
      -INT_MAX,
      rray_max_int_one_na_rm
    );
  } else {
    if (na_rm) {
      RRAY_LOCATE_ACROSS(
        int,
        r_int_cbegin,
        r_int_begin,
        R_TYPE_integer,
        rray_int_is_missing,
        r_globals.na_int,
        rray_locate_max_int_one_na_rm
      );
    } else {
      RRAY_LOCATE_ACROSS(
        int,
        r_int_cbegin,
        r_int_begin,
        R_TYPE_integer,
        rray_int_is_missing,
        -INT_MAX,
        rray_locate_max_int_one
      );
    }
  }
}

For lanes, max and the double min reuse the reducers in one-extremum.h. Int and logical min get a small helper of their own, because rray_min_int_one_na_rm() doesn't vectorize:

static inline int rray_locate_min_int_lane(int best, int x) {
  x = rray_int_is_missing(x) ? INT_MAX : x;
  return x < best ? x : best;
}

The across comparisons are the same as before, but with bitwise helpers:

static inline bool rray_locate_max_int_one(int x, int best) {
  return bool_bitwise_and(
    !rray_int_is_missing(best),
    bool_bitwise_or(rray_int_is_missing(x), x > best)
  );
}

static inline bool rray_locate_max_dbl_one_na_rm(double x, double best) {
  return bool_bitwise_and(!rray_dbl_is_missing(x), !(x <= best));
}

The rest of the diff

  • The kernel signature is now (x, na_rm, it, out). Only the across path needs best, so it allocates it there and rray_locate() no longer does.

  • src/locate.c now includes one-extremum.h.

  • src/decl/locate-decl.h has the new declarations.

  • tests/testthat/test-locate.R has a new test that signed zeros tie, on both paths.

If you'd rather read it on GitHub, I can commit and push it to the PR, and the mobile app will show the diff there. Want me to?

Davis

I’m so surprised you can vectorize position accumulation in across but not lane? That seems nuts

Claude

It's not as strange as it sounds. In the across loop clang isn't vectorizing a position accumulation at all, because each iteration works on its own output slot.

Across

Within one run, iteration i reads and writes v_best[out_loc] and v_out[out_loc], and out_loc is different on every iteration. Nothing that iteration 5 computes is used by iteration 6. So clang can run 4 iterations side by side, one per output slot. position is the same for the whole run, so it's just one value copied into all 4. To the compiler it looks like an element-wise pmax() that also writes an index.

Lane

Every iteration updates the same best and position, so iteration 6 can't start until iteration 5 has finished. To vectorize that, clang has to recognize the loop as a reduction: split it into 4 partial (best, position) pairs, then merge them at the end. It knows how to do that for sum, min, max and or, where merging the partial results is trivial. For "max and the first index of the max", the merge also has to break ties by the smallest index. Apple clang 17 doesn't recognize that pattern. Newer LLVM has been adding support for argmin and argmax style loops, but I'm not sure how far that has got.

You could do it by hand

You'd keep 4 or 16 partial (best, position) slots and stride through the lane. That is the across loop, run over a small buffer, plus a merge step that breaks ties by the smallest position. It's the "split the lane into slots" option from before, which I passed on because the merge and the tail make it more code than two passes.

So the across loop is the hand-vectorized form, with one slot per output. Lanes don't get that for free because they only have one output.

Doubles are a separate problem. Even a plain max along a lane stays scalar, because clang won't reorder a double compare-and-select reduction without -ffast-math.

Davis

What does Numpy do

Claude

NumPy does the "slots" version I passed on, written by hand with SIMD intrinsics. This is from memory of NumPy's source, not something I checked today.

One lane (argfunc.dispatch.c.src)

For contiguous int and float data, NumPy has hand-written SIMD for argmax() and argmin(), built on its own cross-platform intrinsics layer. It doesn't rely on the compiler to vectorize. Roughly:

  • It keeps a vector of the best values so far and a vector of their indices, one pair per SIMD slot.

  • For each block it loads x, compares it with the best so far, and uses one select to update both the values and the indices. That's our across loop with one output per SIMD slot.

  • At the end it finds the largest value across the slots, then the smallest index among the slots that hold it. That's the tie-break merge.

  • For floats, it watches for NaN. As soon as it sees one, it switches to a scalar loop to find the first NaN, since NumPy returns the position of the first NaN.

  • There are also limits on how large indices can get, so it works in blocks and handles short arrays with plain scalar code.

Other axes

As far as I remember, _PyArray_ArgMinMaxCommon() transposes the array so the reduced axis comes last. It then makes a contiguous copy and runs the one-lane function on each lane. So NumPy turns every axis into the lane case and pays for a full copy of the array to do it.

What that means for us

  • Our across path has no copy, and it vectorizes because its shape is already right. So for axes other than the first we should be ahead of NumPy's approach.

  • For lanes, NumPy shows that a single-pass slot version is the way to go further, at the cost of a merge step. It also has one advantage over our two passes that I underplayed before: the slot loop updates each slot on its own, so it isn't a reduction, and the -ffast-math problem doesn't apply. Clang should vectorize it for doubles too. Doubles are where our lanes are weakest now (39 ms → 12 ms, still scalar).

I could prototype a slot-based lane loop in plain C, with no intrinsics, to see whether clang vectorizes it for doubles and how it compares with two passes. It would be more code than the current lane macro. Want me to try it before we commit anything?

Davis

Turn out stride 0 back into the across case and merge the two macros again, keeping what we learned about the across case. Accept the performance hit on the lane case for now. Commit and push that

Claude

I'll start again from the committed src/locate.c, which already has the "one macro, stride 0 branch plus across branch" shape. Then I'll apply only the across lessons:

  • read every value into a local before storing,

  • step out_loc by 1,

  • use bitwise comparisons,

  • read v_out[i] into a local in the final NA pass.

The across branch can't share a loop with stride 0. A stride only known at run time is what stopped it vectorizing, so the merged macro keeps both inner loops.

I committed and pushed the merged macro to the PR as ae30a2e. Everything passes (3740 tests), and the benchmark against the commit before it is running now.

There is one macro again, RRAY_LOCATE(), with its two inner loops picked by out_stride. Both loops have to stay: a single loop with a stride known only at run time is what stopped the across loop from vectorizing.

What changed from the previous commit

  • The across loop reads best and out into locals before storing, steps out_loc by 1, and uses bitwise comparisons. It now vectorizes for every type, including doubles.

  • The final NA pass reads v_out[i] into a local first, so it vectorizes too.

  • The stride 0 loop is back to the version from the previous commit. Two things went with it: the two-pass lane code and the extra min helpers. one-extremum.h is no longer included, and the decl header ends up unchanged.

  • I kept the signed zero test.

The commit message is the single line Vectorize the across loops of rray_locate_max() and rray_locate_min().

The lane cost you accepted

The stride 0 loop is still scalar, and it now uses the bitwise comparisons too. In the earlier round that made lanes 1.2x slower for int and logical with no missing values, and up to 5.8x slower with missing values (int and logical max with na_rm = FALSE). Doubles were unaffected. The benchmark will confirm the actual numbers for this commit.

Write ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/project_locate_bitwi… 
---
name: project-locate-bitwise-slower
description: "Locate loops: across loop vectorizes with hoisted loads, unit stride and bitwise; lane (stride 0) loop stays scalar and bitwise slows it"
metadata:
  type: project
---

On 2026-10-07 (PR #134, commit ae30a2e) the across branch of `RRAY_LOCATE()` in `src/locate.c` was vectorized by three changes together: reading `best` and `out` into locals before storing, stepping `out_loc` by 1 instead of a runtime `out_stride` (always 1 there), and bitwise `rray_locate_*_one()` helpers. Across cases became 1x to 13x faster. With `&&`, or with a runtime stride, it stays scalar.

The stride 0 (lane) loop stays scalar, since clang has no argmax reduction. Bitwise helpers make it slower than `&&` (int/lgl max with `na_rm = FALSE` and missing values: 3 ms to 16 ms), because `&&` keeps a cheap branch that stops loading `x` after the first `NA`. Davis accepted that cost to keep one macro.

**Why:** a lane two-pass version (vectorized max, then branch-free backward search) was 2x to 6x faster for lanes but judged too complex. NumPy vectorizes lanes with per-slot best/index vectors plus a tie-break merge.

**How to apply:** if lane speed comes back up, the candidates are the two-pass loop or a NumPy style slot loop (which should also vectorize doubles). See [[project-loop-alignment-noise]].
The file ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/project_locate_bitwise_slower.md has been updated successfully.

-Locate loops vectorize with hoisted loads plus bitwise (across lanes) and two passes (along a lane)
+Locate across loop vectorizes (hoisted loads, unit stride, bitwise); lane loop stays scalar, options noted
The file ~/.claude/projects/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/memory/MEMORY.md has been updated successfully.

ae30a2e makes reductions along any axis except the first much faster, and the lane cost you accepted is about what I expected.

Across (any axis except the first)

  • Every case is 1.0x to 13x faster. The geometric mean is 0.27x to 0.35x for each type, so about 3x to 4x faster.

  • Shapes with long runs gain the most. For example, 1e6 x 10 along axis 2 is 0.12x to 0.18x.

  • Shapes whose runs are only 10 long gain least, at about 0.8x.

Lanes (axis 1, or a plain vector)

  • Doubles are a bit faster, at 0.91x. The cleaned-up across loop probably helps the shared parts.

  • Int and logical are 1.08x to 1.29x slower by geometric mean.

  • The worst cases are int and logical max with na_rm = FALSE and 10% NA: 3 ms becomes 16 ms, about 5.5x slower. This is the case where && let the old loop stop reading x once a lane hit NA.

  • The next worst is int and logical min with na_rm = TRUE: 9 ms becomes 14 ms, about 1.6x slower.

  • 10-element lanes got a little faster (0.8x).

Clean up

  • I removed the two temporary worktrees I made. The other /tmp worktrees were there before this session, so I left them.

  • I cleared the compiled code so your next load_all() builds without the alignment flag.

  • I updated the memory note with where this ended up. It also records the two ways to speed lanes back up later: the two-pass version, or a NumPy-style slot loop, which should vectorize doubles too.
